ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u1	Good morning. The session is open. The agenda is published in the Official Gazette of Parliament, so we do not proceed to its reading. The president of the board, Mr. Alberto Núñez Feijóo, justifies the absence of this plenary meeting because of the meeting held in Asturias and attended by the presidents of Castela-León, the Principality of Asturias and Galicia, which is held today at 12 a.m. We start with the agenda, legislative texts. Debates and votes of the Ditome, delivered by Commission 8th, Fisheries and Marisqueo, of the draft port law of Galicia
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u2	The amendments of the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego, the Socialist Parliamentary Group and the Parliamentary Group of En Marea are kept for discussion. The president of Commission 8th, Fisheries and Marisqueo, Marta Rodríguez Arias, has the floor for the presentation of the dictation.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u3	Thank you, President. Ladies and gentlemen, the bill was entered into Parliament's Register on 28 August 2017. The Bureau of Parliament, at its meeting on 29 August 2017, issued its publication in the Official Bulletin of the Parliament of Galicia and the opening of the deadline for the submission of amendments. Even so, listening to the Speaker's Board, he agreed to the 8th Commission, Fisheries, Marisqueo and Aquaculture. The parliamentary groups of En Marea, the Galician Socialists and the Galician Nationalist Block each presented amendments to the whole. They were qualified on 20 September 2017 and rejected at the plenary session on 17 October 2017. The Group of the People's Parliamentary Group presented 2, the Group of En Marea, 53; the Socialist Group, 177; and the Group of the Galician Nationalist Block, 106, which was qualified on October 17, 2017. Commission 8th, at its meeting on 24 October, appointed the following address: Popular Group: Don Teresa Egerique Mosquera, holder; Don Carlos Enrique López Crespo, alternate. Group of En Marea: Don Juan José Merlo Lorenzo, holder; Dona Carmele Santos Queiruga, alternate. Parliamentary Group of the Socialists of Galicia: María Dolores Toja Suárez, holder; Fr Julio Torrado Quintela, alternate. Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego: Don Maria Monserrat Prado Cores, holder; Don Xosé Luis Rivas Cruz, alternate. The session was held on 10 and 14 November 2017. The amendments to the joint text of the bill were studied in them, and the report containing the recommendations made to the Commission, consisting of the majority of the amendments made by the People's Parliamentary Group. It also recommended the acceptance of Amendment No 39 from the Parliamentary Group of En Marea; Amendments Nos 7, 35, 40, 49, 53, 54, 55, 57, 64, 106, 114, 130, 135, 141, 142, 151, 161 and 166 from the Parliamentary Group of Socialists; Amendment No 82 from the Parliamentary Group of the BNG; and Amendment No 39 from the Parliamentary Group of Socialists. There were pending possible transactions in Amendment No 70 to the Parliamentary Group of the BNG, and Amendment No 167 to the Parliamentary Group of the Socialists. Finally, the Phoenician Government, for the most part, recommended the rejection of the rest. The 8th Commission, meeting on 23 November 2017, approved by most of the opinion that the plenary debate should take place. The ditame consists of an explanatory statement, followed by 147 articles, 7 additional provisions, 8 transitional provisions, 1 derogation provision and 2 final provisions. The articulate thickens in a preliminary title, followed by six more titles. The preliminary title refers to the general provisions; Title I regulates the port organisation of Galicia; Title II addresses the planning, management, works, environment and security; Title III refers to the public domain of the port; Title IV is dedicated to the ports and harbour areas of nautical and sports use; Title V regulates the general scheme for the provision of port services and the development of commercial, industrial or other activities; and Title VI refers to the regulation of operation and police, the powers, inspection and security and the regime of sanctions. Finally, the law is expected to enter into force at six months of its publication in the Official Journal of Galicia. Thank you.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u4	Thank you, Mrs. Rodríguez Arias. We therefore begin the round of parliamentary groups to set their position on the dictatorship and, if so, defend the amendments maintained. Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego, Mrs Prado.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u5	Good morning. Galicia has a large number of ports and port facilities — the highest port density on the Iberian peninsula, probably the highest in Europe — which make the port sector of Galicia a strategic sector of great potential and which has so far been regulated by a specific law since 1981, where the Statute of Autonomy in force included exclusive competition in the sector for Galicia, leaving out the so-called ports of general interest. And in a nation like the Galician, port infrastructures are therefore of vital importance. This law seeks to regulate activity in the 122 port infrastructures of exclusive competence of Galicia. And it would be normal, in a normal country, where democracy was something more than a word, which, with the transcendence of what is being regulated in this first Galician Harbour Act, the People's Party had a desire to approach, dialogue, to listen to contributions, to the views that we have of the political organizations represented in this Parliament as regards the port infrastructures that we have. But far from that reality, the People's Party, after voting against amendments to the whole and in the debate process of partial amendments, it was no longer that the knee had passed, but it had made it a real lousy. The Galician Nationalist Block introduced 106 partial amendments and the People's Party accepted a word; it accepted an amendment to a word: where it had a "constructive project" that would become called a "execution project", because it was obviously a bad approach that originally had the law. That is, 106 amendments, and the supporter of the People's Party accepts—of these 106 amendments from the Nationalist Block of Wales—a word. Dialogue, consensus, agreement, listening... Where are those words, ladies and gentlemen of the People's Party? Clearly, they don't exist in your dictionary. We present 106 amendments to try to modify, improve many aspects that, as they are collected by law, are tremendously harmful and harmful. They want our amendments to correct some fundamental aspects such as the transfer to Galicia of the so-called ports of general interest, the control from Galicia of port activity, the sharing of the municipalities in planning and decision-making, the linkage of the economic-portuarial activity to those related to the sea and the strictly so-called port activity, the prevalence of fishing activity on nautical-sports, environmental guarantees, the prevalence of public interest on the private, coordination mechanisms, workers' labour rights, the regulation of traditional boats, the spaces for traditional boats on the docks, the limitation of fillings and gender equality in the management bodies. And, in view of these issues which seem obvious that they are not minors — it seems obvious that we are talking about matters of vital importance — the answer was the overwhelming support of the People's Party. Because this law responds to the ideological project of the People's Party of Liberalization, of profit and business profit instead of public interest and general interest. It is clear in the law — and the rapporteur, Mrs Egerique, has also explained it — that the amendments to the BNG cannot be accepted because they respond to a different model. Of course! Of course! Because, in view of the submission to Madrid of the People's Party's law, the Galician decision that proposes the Galego Nationalist Block; because, in view of the private interest of this People's Party law, the public interest that defends the Galician Nationalist Block; because, in view of the order and command of the People's Party's law, coordination and dialogue that defends the Galician Nationalist Block. It's a clearly privatizing, liberalizing law and strategic economic activity in Galicia. It is literally stated that public activity will be launched whenever necessary to address possible deficiencies in private activity. I believe that the approach of the law is already practically summarized in this. It doesn't correct and even accentuates the privatization tensions the sector is facing. The words privatization and business benefit are those that govern this Ports Act. And two examples: first, to choose from existing legal figures the corporate public entity to call the Galician Ports agency. Choose this figure, of course, to escape as much as possible from public control, to maximize the objective of obtaining business results and benefit, to be more free to apply autonomous commercial and business policies for the results account of each port, but nothing to do with the public control of it. That's why BNG has tabled an amendment that, instead of being in front of a public enterprise, we are in front of an autonomous body, in order to ensure that there is maximum public control, from the public administration, of this enterprise. Another element that clearly simplifies this objective of privatization and business benefit is the extension of concessions to 50 years. The normal thing, of course, from the BNG point of view, would be to apply the precautionary principle, and a thirty-year concession seems to us more than reasonable for a concession, and so that, at the end of which, the assessment of how that concession came about. That is why we also tabled amendments to maintain the 30-year period for port concessions. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u12	Thank you very much, Mrs. Toja. Mr Merlo Lorenzo has the floor on the part of the Parliamentary Group of En Marea.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u13	Thank you very much, President. Ladies and gentlemen, today we are going to debate the Galician Ports Bill. We always felt that the need to give this project ahead, the need for a Galician Port Law for these more than 120 ports of competition, was very necessary, given the dispersion of existing regulations in these matters and many other factors. Unfortunately — and on that line there were many of our amendments — this law is not directed, precisely, to solving these kinds of issues or trying to solve many other problems, which are very necessary, precisely, for the port management of these more than 120 ports. Precisely the need to establish that integral exploitation of the set of ports of autonomous ownership, to harmonise and distribute the uses and activities and to coordinate even the economic activity of these ports and those of general interest, which are often sharing even the laughter itself, is a matter which this bill completely forgets. It's a bill, therefore, unable to initiate joint and coordinated planning, avoid unfair competences and enable a country strategy to be established in the port area. This is a question we're suffering and we're going to suffer, because this law doesn't hurt or solve it. The rule also leaves much aside, from the point of view of our parliamentary group, the resolution of the ongoing conflicts between the port authorities and the municipalities, regardless of the political colour that is at each moment. It was a good question to try to solve with this bill, but it was neither active nor passive. This project clearly invades municipal competences and does not facilitate this cooperation of which I spoke between Ports and Town Councils, especially in matters such as the treatment and use of maritime facades. We have to bear in mind, precisely, that these types of ports have a much more important integration with the towns that host them than the ports of general interest, and therefore this was a much more important issue that had been dealt with in this law, and it is not touched. Then we also have the question that the determination of the executive bodies, as is envisaged, in charge of decision-making, of the execution of the agreements, and the determination of the members of that board of directors, does not seem to be very balanced. It forgets the presence of relevant organizations, as for us may be producer organisations or trade unions, and does not quite clearly determine the mechanisms for their presence or not, in addition to obviating a part of organised civil society or the necessary municipal representation, and to increase and direct a large number of competences in bodies of one person's character, which are against the need to socialize initiatives in port policy. That is to say, it is once again a feud closed in which there are possibly many disputes and a lot of confrontation about the interests and use of this kind of port, in which the nature of services must always dominate rather than the business character, which is what this bill is all about. It is also excessively abused — and it seems very serious — since we are going to draw a law that has been demanded for a long time by the sector and by the Greek society; it is a law that is necessary for the port development of the country — from the post-development use of decrees and regulations to determine fundamental elements, from the priorities of port uses, rates, public prices, etc., and therefore clearly prevents a minimal planning or previous direction which compromises a line of action and which can be shared, for example, by legislative bodies. We cannot develop and move initiatives on this bill because it really all depends on the regulations that develop later in fundamental and important matters. So he misses all of that, which many amendments were trying to move, and these kind of questions are open. Finally, and without deepening much, the explanatory statement of this bill establishes a subordination of the public function of the ports to the private initiative. This fact is reflected in numerous precepts that contain this project, where the private initiative is preceded by the public management of the activity, from uses, concessions, administrative authorizations... it therefore subsides public interest to the particular economic interest, and moreover establishes mechanisms for the authorisation and granting of the water sheet for the private initiative which may come to collide with the neighbouring uses of marine and fisheries exploitation. We understand that several articles of the project we tabled amendments abused the time of concessions. The same fifty years seemed to us very much, given the times of depreciation of investments that could be made in connection with port infrastructures or private investments that had been amortised in fifty years; it's excessive. None of these time-related amendments, in which we underestimated something that we considered reasonable, in most cases thirty years, instead of fifty, were accepted. Nor was it accepted a question which for us was fundamental, which was to try to prevent the use of structures and ports in matters as sensitive to many populations as waste incinerators; that is, this law leaves entirely open — and our amendment, without being restrictive, went a little along that line — that the incinerations of waste products from maritime activity could only be treated like this. This law leaves open the possibility that any waste, any waste, may be incinerated, and in addition to the branch of the energy cogeneration plants, which will naturally be removed from Natural Gas. But those amendments are not accepted. So we're in front of a law that allows a priori, even if the will is not that, that anyone can set up a marine urban waste incineration plant on a harbour line. We didn't want to accept that kind of amendment, and we thought it wasn't restrictive. So, and given that only one amendment has been accepted from us, and by way of a matter mainly, we cannot support this kind of law, because we do believe that this type of law is not the law that will allow rational development and that will allow, precisely, synergies and cooperation between the ports of Greece. It is a very necessary issue, given the crisis that is going through the sector, and we must try to move forward to be more competitive, having one of the fundamental pillars of our Greek economy, especially on the coastline. We are losing a very great opportunity, and we are depending — I say — on the will of those who lay down regulations and decrees that really shape the uses and issues that reach all that this law leaves out. So we can't support her. Nothing more and thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u14	Thank you very much, Mr. Merlo. The People's Parliamentary Group in Galicia has the floor, Mrs Egerique Mosquera.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u15	Thank you, President. Good morning, gentlemen. Today, finally, after a long journey — which has been said here that you have been working for a long time in this law — I insist — making a metaphor — that you arrive at the right port, reaches Galicia, and arrives well, the first autonomous port law, a law that regulates the management of the 122 dependent ports of the Greek administration; 122 ports with very different realities — hence the difficulty also to fit this legislative text. Realitys different from the activities they develop in them and the towns where each of these ports is located. Although today the spokeswoman of the Nationalist Block Galego here spoke, in most of his intervention, of the ports of state ownership, today we speak of the first autonomous law that regulates the 122 autonomous ports. This law, which we are going to adopt today in the Galician Parliament, will allow port legislation to be adapted to the various peculiarities of the Greek docks; particularities to which state legislation cannot comply. That's why we are convinced, despite his speech, that this is evident proof that today we are strengthening our self-government. With the approval of this autonomous port law, Galician self-government is reinforced. This law takes into account, as I have said, the coexistence of the various activities in most ports, which are fishing activity, commercial activity, nautical activity, transit activity and also cultural-marital activity. Because it is not true that we have completely rejected the inclusion — certainly, in Article 54 — of cultural and maritime uses. For the elaboration of this law, Portos de Galicia and the Council of the Sea sought consensus at all times, and for this reason we found approaches to different areas linked to the sea, linked to the ports, which defined the pillars on which this legislative text had to be established, with a main objective, which is to strengthen the potential of the ports of Greece in the face of the challenges of the future. Because it's not true that it's an outlawed law, that it's a law based on the past, because it's a law that faces challenges in the future. In Galicia we have to aspire from our ports to open up new markets, we have to aspire from our ports to find new investors and we have to aspire from our ports to take out as much performance as possible to the Welsh port sector; a yield that affects the benefit of the people working in the sea sector, not as it has been said here, and a benefit that affects the marine villages, which, by extension, is the benefit of all Galicia. We're talking about greater competitiveness with this law. We speak of greater competitiveness with the absolute guarantee of fulfilling its role as public service; absolute assurance of fulfilling its role as public service. Ports are not privatized, as much as they want to say and repeat them, ports are not privatized. Self-employed ports will be able to count on new investors, of course, who, by providing services of activities such as mooring, trailering, transporting of passengers or handling of cargo, will contribute to generating jobs and will contribute to reactivating the economy and port activity in the areas and villages in which they are located. So neither goes to a completely different part of the council, but this law is intended to strengthen economic activity and strengthen the level of employment of marine villages as well. We're talking about this law of greater transparency. The fundamental law on transparency in procedures in the granting of public port services, which recovers competition from projects, requests for concessions, with the provision of public information from official bodies and bodies, will enable the best decisions to be made in the interests of the general interest. As far as port traffic is concerned, a substantial improvement is foreseen. Why is substantial improvement expected with this law? Because the new law foresees the shape of the harbour infrastructure managers' plans, and that doesn't have to be something negative, quite the opposite. These plans will plan the allocation of projects of works and installations for a time horizon of at least five years, another guarantee for the Greek ports. These plans can also be formulated for a port or a set of them to optimize resources. And what will they allow? They will make it possible to speed up current port situations, the structure of traffic in port activities, their development needs and other technical issues which will improve the conditions of delivery and facilities. We talk about ports and we talk about competitiveness, we don't talk about politics or intentions or concrete political interests. On the other hand, special plans. Special plans also collect this law, and collect the system of each port and the activities that can be developed in it, including the forecasting measures necessary to ensure efficient exploitation of port space and its connection to general terrestrial transport systems. We are also talking about the future because we are talking about greater sustainability, we are talking about environmental sustainability, about prevention and combating pollution, and we are talking about the possibility that, in any public harbour domain, in the event of a solid, liquid or gaseous discharge, the obligation for those responsible for these spills to fully regenerate the waters. In addition, the withdrawal of abandoned vessels, as well as vehicles and other objects on port land will be accelerated at last. A small incision to expand information: the law considers abandoned a ship that has already taken more than six months in the same place without appreciable activity and has not updated the payment of fees. In the case of vehicles, machinery and objects in general, ports of Galicia may declare them in a situation of abandonment whenever they stay in the port for more than a month in the same place and without activity. So, we're here at a law that guarantees agility in becoming daily and at work and in the daily business of Galician ports; speed in deadlines and also in the authorizations of seizures depending on the vacancies docking squares. These procedures will not exceed the fifteen working days, and, in case of high demand, a waiting list will be created with more defined criteria for access to the trap. Agility also in concessions. The procedure for granting concessions will be faster and will include greater guarantees for investors, with a decisive role, as I said earlier, of companies interested in generating jobs and wealth and taking forward and making our ports in Galicia competitive. So what is the four-month recruitment process could be reduced to one month, facilitating activity and simplifying bureaucracy, something that demands the vast majority of the Greeks and the Greeks. We saw in some of the amendments to specific groups that extended or reduced deadlines, that complicated the creation of agencies of other councils, which made this very difficult and even delayed the whole bureaucracy. And really what the sector is asking for, what the Greeks and the Greeks are asking for, is the simplification of bureaucracy and the agility in administrative procedures. Another demand from the sector, and in line with Royal Decree Law 8/2014, of 4 July, of urgent measures for growth, competitiveness and efficiency, was that of enlargement in the time of the harbour concessions, which with this law range from a minimum of thirty years to a maximum of 50 years. They've all denied this enlargement, they don't see that this enlargement is favorable, but it makes it easier to amortize investments and makes them more attractive. I insist, not always from a negative point of view, but in order to amortize, to do, to set up activities in the ports and to set up activities in the towns and to make from the ports economic motors in the towns where they are found. I would also like to point out that the new Ports Act entitles the staff that is watching and watching for security in the ports the character of the authority agent. That is, they will guard them, they are now being strengthened to ensure security in ports where they carry out their activities, and they can exercise records in the case of bad practices and detect with greater certainty and guarantee those who do not comply with the rules. The truth is we're talking about a reality of 122 very different ports, and as an example I've already put on commission, for example, that of Cambados or that of Celero. But we can see in every port of 122 that great differences are established between them. So the important thing about this law is that it will improve the day by day and the daily routine of work in those ports. In conclusion, and from my point of view, this legislative text is called upon to meet three objectives: firstly, it would be to establish the regulatory, organisational and collaborative framework within the whole network port infrastructure of Galicia — that is one of the main objectives — the second would be to increase the synergies of the ports of Greece in the transport of goods, because we can still improve much more than it is already done; and thirdly, to coordinate competences in port investment, transport and management of the territory. Even though other intentions are seen here, as I say, and they are always negative in the future, this will allow, in addition to being consensual, ports to better adapt to the becoming and daily routine of the towns and to improve and be a revolutionary to the economy of those villages. We are talking about the rule that will regulate the management, operation and administration regime of the port system in a manner adapted to the actual needs of the confrries, to the real needs of nautical clubs, operators, investors, entrepreneurs providing port activity; ultimately, of the whole sector, because we all have a place and we can't forget anyone because of prejudice, which sometimes have no basis for rejecting them. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u30	Thank you, President. Good morning. The proposed law, on a popular legislative initiative, of measures to guarantee energy as a public service and against energy poverty, was introduced in the past legislation in the Register of Parliament on 19 May 2015, with registration number 37326. It contains the articulated text and an exposition of motives for this initiative, as well as the reasons for its processing. The Bureau of Parliament, at its meeting on 22 June 2015, admitted this initiative, declared its commission to promote it and agreed to notify this agreement to the Galician Election Board, for the purposes laid down in Articles 7 and 8 of Law 1/1988 of 19 January, regulating popular legislative initiatives before the Galician Parliament. The plenary session of Parliament took into consideration at the meeting on January 26, 2016, following the presentation and defense by Mr. Carlos Fernando Branco Parga, appointed by the commission to promote these purposes. The Bureau of Parliament, at its meeting on 15 November 2016, agreed to entrust the Bureau of Commission 6th, Industry, Energy, Trade and Tourism with the management of the processing of this proposal for a popular legislative initiative law and to leave pending the opening of the deadline for the submission of amendments to the articulate. After the processing of various documentation and after the completion of the appearances of several experts in Commission 6th, the Bureau, at its meeting on 19 September 2017, agreed to the opening of the deadline for submitting amendments to the articulated, which, after their extensions, qualified by the Commission Bureau on 30 October 2017, were published in the Official Bulletin of the Galician Parliament No. 203, of 31 October 2017, with the following detail: amendments to the articulated: a total of 54. In document 19482 of the Galego Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group, 2 amendments; in document 19484 of the Galician Socialist Group, 17 amendments; in document 19485 of the En Marea Parliamentary Group, 23 amendments; and in document 19488 of the Galician People's Group, 12 amendments. For its part, the Ponency designated in Commission 6th, Industry, Energy, Trade and Tourism of 3 November 2017 is composed of the following speakers: For the People's Parliamentary Group, Marta Nóvoa Iglesias as holder and don Moisés Blanco Paradelo as alternate; for the Parliamentary Group of En Marea, Fr Francisco Casal Vidal as holder and Fr Juan José Merlo Lorenzo as alternate; for the Parliamentary Group of the Socialists of Galicia, Don Abel Fermín Losada Álvarez as holder and Don Noela Blanco Rodríguez as alternate; for the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego, Don Noa Presas Bergantiños as holder and don María Monserrat Prado Cores as alternate. He held his constitutive session on November 3, 2017, with the assistance of the keynote speakers: Mrs. Presas Bergantinos, Mr. Losada Álvarez, Mr. Casal Vidal and Mrs. Novoa Iglesias. At the following meetings, the Pôncia studied the whole of this bill, as well as the amendments to the articulate presented, in the search for the approximation of the different positions. Finally, at the last meeting, which took place on 15 November, Parliament prepared its report. Subsequently, the 6th Commission, at its meeting on 23 November 2017, adopted its ruling, with 7 votes in favour, 3 against and 2 abstentions, incorporating in the bill, in addition to a technical amendment, a total of three amendments from the Socialist Parliamentary Group, two of which were negotiated. Following the withdrawal of two amendments from the En Marea Parliamentary Group, the remaining amendments rejected in this plenary are maintained: from the En Marea Parliamentary Group, 21, from the Socialists Parliamentary Group, 13; and from the Galego Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group, 2. Finally, it emerges that the bill consists, in addition to its explanatory statement, of a preliminary title and two titles, developed in 16 articles: the preliminary title establishes the object of the law, the scope and general principles; Title I is dedicated to energy saving and efficiency; and Title II covers the guarantee of accessibility to energy. The final part is incorporated into an additional provision, a derogation and two finals; one of which establishes the entry into force of this law, if applicable, January 1, 2018. It is therefore necessary to complete the processing of this legislative procedure with the approval, if any, of the Proposal for a Law, of a popular legislative initiative, of measures to ensure energy as a public service and against energy poverty.

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u42	Good morning, everyone. We're going to talk today about one of the greatest injustices and a violation of the rights that are taking place in our country: energy poverty, the result of the growing inequality and the unacceptable conjugation of public authorities in Spain with the electric oligopolie. Energy poverty is undoubtedly associated with poverty in general, but there are, however, reasons for emphasis on this concept of poverty, and the main thing is that in Galicia energy poverty has grown much more than poverty in general in recent years. The data we have is that between 2007 and 2013 households in general poverty multiplied by two in Galicia, while households in energy poverty multiplied by three, reaching 10% in 2014. In any case, the latter percentages are set for households with an income available not above the income of insertion, as we know, 531 euros — I think they are — after paying the cost of living. But the most extensive international legislation is that a home is considered to be in energy poverty when it has to spend more than 10% of its net income to pay the necessary energy in homes, after the cost of housing, after taking out the rent or mortgage. If we apply this international approach recognised by all international institutions that affect these types of situations, we would reach that 20 per cent of the Greek homes, more than 600,000 people, would be in Galicia now in energy poverty. Another of the main reasons for energy poverty is the increase in electricity bill, 76% in the crisis period, when in many other European countries the increase has not passed by 5%, as is reasonable. Consumption's down. When consumption goes down, prices go down in almost every market. Well, no, Spain has increased by 76%, while family income has continued to decline. All the electricity legislation of the last 20 years has been on the line to protect this real scam, which is the price of electricity in Spain, the most expensive in Europe before taxes. After taxes, we're the fourth, but electricity taxes, although people don't know, in countries like Denmark and others, are much higher. In Spain, the share of taxes on the receipt of light is around 20%, and in Denmark, for example, reaches 60% of the total price. But what's the matter? That the cost of production and access is much lower than in Spain. The setting of the production price in Spain is dark and intolerable; only by removing the nuclear and hydraulic production part of the pool that determines daily the cost of kilowatt/hour, as is done in virtually all of Europe, could a drop of these costs by about 20%. I'm what everyone knows -- and sometimes they don't know why -- like the so-called fallen benefits from the sky for electricity; that is to say, to be able to charge the kilowatts that ever cost anything or cost about 10 or 12 euros megavattio/hour, as it happens in nuclear, to 60 or 70 euros, as it is going on these days, because of this invention of the day-to-day pool that is being done in Spain, when this is not being done practically anywhere. On the other hand, in the rest of Europe there is much more real competition from marketers who press producers to lower prices at reasonable profit levels, as the Socialist spokesman has already said. But this allowed vertical integration of production, distribution and marketing. They tell us they are different companies: Gas Natural Fenosa production, Gas Natural Fenosa Distribution, Gas Natural Fenosa Marketing. Obviously, they are different companies. This, of course, prevents real competition, which is not happening, for example, in Germany. The pressure of marketers, including municipal and regional, is too strong for producers to have to really reflect the real cost of production at the price. Also in this country and in the rest of Spain, self-consumption is attacked, which would leave a lot of energy to the daily market and take it down to prices. If you hadn't entered the daily market so much energy — at this point around 80% of the energy we consume every day — then prices, of course — because the power offered would remain the same and consumption would be lower — would go down. But this is not allowed. The Government of the People's Party, as we all know, resolutely combats self-consumption, so as not to put at risk the power of large companies to set totally disproportionate prices that fatten their results accounts, spoil families and make our industry less competitive. And we're not talking about the regular part anymore. I'd rather call it a regulated part because many people call it beaches, but most of the costs reflected in the regulated part have nothing to do with access fees, they have nothing to do with transport, which is a very small cost, have nothing to do with distribution or with other costs actually applicable to access to energy. Most are really another type of tax, like, for example, the invention of transition to competition; an invention to give billions of euros to electricity with the concept that competition — the competition that I was talking about before — would lower the prices that were paid for production. The reality was the opposite. The prices of production continued to rise unmeasuredly and yet billions were paid to electricity for the cost of translation to competition. Then there is the valuation of the distribution costs. If they were actually audited independently, they would be extremely important savings. In our estimates, in the calculations that we're doing, we calculated that we could go down on 40%, over 3,000 million euros a year, especially in countries like Galicia, where a very important part of the mid-voltage distribution network has already been paid by all the Greeks in the Mega Plan that developed in the 1990s and 2,000. This was really important savings, so In Marea defends public control of the distribution, just like transport. This would open up the possibility, for example, as is done in Germany, that distributors would be regional levels, or even comarcas or locals; that would indeed open the possibility of differentiated rates. The current situation in the Spanish State would not open up the possibility of differentiated tariffs, according to a very taxing regulation of the European Union. Before that, we should take control, as with transport, which doesn't mean expropriation; it's public control of the management of distributors. After that, we continue with gifts, which we all, in the end, pay in our receipt. Against these billions and billions, which if we add up to the order of EUR 15 billion a year that is given to the electricians, there is the cigarette industry to fight energy poverty, with an estimate that in the decree of the social bond it is about EUR 300 million a year and that in Galicia they could add, with which the People's Party lords are presenting to us, just over EUR 300 million. Energy poverty, in addition, has enormous health consequences: 7,000 premature deaths are estimated in Galicia due to problems of energy poverty, and have a very serious impact on social and educational issues. Young people and adolescents who live in homes with energy poverty have much stronger barriers in order to develop in the educational field, and it is demonstrated — and many teachers say so — that they have an impact on the performance of these students who live in this kind of home. Energy poverty, while poverty, is not just a social or economic dysfunction, it is mainly an ethical problem that attacks the dignity of every human being. All of this also makes us reflect on the contradiction of reality with what is written in the Spanish Constitution. For example, Article 45 states that public authorities will ensure the rational use of all natural resources in order to protect and improve the quality of life and to defend and restore the environment. It also states in Article 51 that public authorities will ensure consumer and user protection by protecting safety, health and legitimate economic interests of consumers and users. So all the liberalizing reforms of successive laws in the electricity sector explicitly eliminated the concept of electricity supply as a public service. All of this, in our view, goes against these two constitutional requirements, and nobody says anything. And let us no longer tell the multitude of European directives that establish the need to protect vulnerable customers and how electricity companies have to have funds available to combat energy poverty. There are four or five directives that explicitly mark it; with which, the State has the right to mark all these processes. The People's Group leaves out of the law all interventions on energy poverty, it really destroys the whole law. This is no longer a law of energy poverty, it's a law of theoretical utterances on energy efficiency, without realising anything, without realising for who, how much money or how much help would be. Indeed, it is a waste of good intentions, without realising at all; that is, it will not have budgetary concretion, nor will it be known that homes will truly have the right to everything that would be the measures that are stated, I say, as a toast to the sun, within the amendments with which the People's Party defeats the whole ILP. That is why, since Marea, we have maintained 21 amendments that basically tend to be true both in people and in homes who would have the right to the social bond — which would be complemented in Galicia — and in the amount of money. And so we thought that we would have to expand the homes that have the right to that bond, because, according to the current proposal, which is the only point the People's Party would keep in what remains of this law, would only reach 5% of the homes that we spoke about before; that is, those that are in extreme situations of vulnerability and social exclusion, leaving out the remaining 95%. Then, we want to make sure that in the available income the income is after the payments of the housing rental costs. This is intolerable, if decrees are passed, if laws are passed, giving the fact that most of the citizenship involved will not access them. Another issue that led us to vote against the social bond is the ridiculous limit of permissible consumption. As you know, the limit that is set, for example, for a person who lives alone in a home — like most people who can access these aids, who would be pensioners, pensioners who live alone in a Welsh home -- is 1,200 kilowatts per hour. So if you make an idea -- and if you don't, when you get home, look at your electricity and gas receipts and look at how much you consume a month, both in electricity and gas in kilowatts/hours -- these 1,200 kilowatts/hour would amount to the following: this lady could plug a half-power stove -- 1,000 watts, 1 kilowatts -- for between 6 and 8 hours a day, five months of winter, November to March. With that half-hour-power stove in a single room in his home, it would already run out of 1,200 kilowatts per hour. What does that involve? The lady's going to have to shower in cold water, she's going to have to light up with candles, she won't have energy to be able to heat up the food, etc.

And then there's another question, which seems to us, of course, more fundamental and important, which is the answer to exceptional emergency situations, which is a slow, uncoordinated and ineffective response, and history is there to prove it. Fandicosta, Angrois, the 2006 fire crisis, the Prestige or the recent fire crisis of October 15th, have highlighted all the failures, discoordinatement and chaos that usually occur when there is an exceptional situation. And that is evidenced by the structural failure of a Greek emergency system, which is characterized, firstly, by disordination, because there is a lack of that single operational command, by fragmentation and multiplicity, both of competences and services — only in the extinction of forest fires there are up to seven different services acting — by precarious labour and means, and also by privatization, which is turning into a private business, which is a fundamental public service, both in the field of ambulances and 112, firefighters, forest fires or maritime rescue. And then it is also evident that something we have already said in the debate of Mr. Rueda's appearance, which is the gross non-compliance of the law — as has been said here today — with the 2007 Emergency and Civil Protection Act, when the Galician Council itself does not meet the needs and mandates of that law that should already be in force in 2010. For example, the regulations for firefighters and voluntary workers, which were to be approved in 2009, or the homogenisation of benefits and working conditions throughout the territory of fire protection and extinction services, which were to be approved in 2011. In addition, we have also made the need to review and update all action plans and protocols, particularly with regard to the training, information, protection and self-protection of the population. In addition to being evidenced in the past, there was a lot of evidence in the past fire crisis of October 15, when the entire system of communication and operational response on the ground failed sharply. And we also draw attention to the fact that with climate change, with the mountain situation, with the urban environment, this kind of crisis, these kinds of disasters are going to continue to produce and more seriously, and we must take note, we must take action. Also of the need for functional and operational integration in the new building of the Road, which cost 8 million euros and which is semi-balary, which is a true expenditure and dilapidation of means and resources. And, therefore, it is necessary to integrate and join all the services and coordinate them effectively and end that tayffic kingdom in this area. In conclusion, what we propose at the BNG is a profound and structural change of the Greek Emergency System, going towards a Greek system with full competence in our country in all areas related to emergencies and rescue, including maritime rescue. Secondly, a public system, with professional and wage approval. Thirdly, a universal system that enables the effective coverage of the entire population and the whole territory. Fourthly, an integral system that effectively addresses all emergencies. And finally, a system coordinated with a single operating command, which we haven't had until now. It's, therefore, a very serious matter. We're talking about a central and vital service for a country such as the saving of goods and, above all, of human lives. And, therefore, we ask that this obsolete system, this precarious system, this ineffective system, be overcome, to convert emergencies into a private business — all this is at the service of privatization and private business — by a public system that guarantees a faster, more effective and safer response to emergencies. Thank you very much.

I want to start by offering a view of the situation of the system in this 2017-2018 course. Each school course involves caring for a large number of people and enabling enough human and material resources. It's a responsibility and an obligation to achieve the best for our boys and girls. This statement is not new, but it must be very present in order to assess reality in its fair measure. The 2017-2018 course started normally. I would say — without any kind of self-comprehensibility — with good or very good note, since the incidences were minimal and were resolved in time and form. We are talking about that every month of September everything has to be in place to meet the educational needs of nearly 400,000 pupils — specifically 383,200 pupils and students — of different levels of non-university education at different points in the Galician geography. Galicia has more than 32,700 students in special education, primary education, ESO and bachelor's degree, to whom nearly 51,000 students are added from a Vocational Training cycle. The number of students in artistic and sports teachings must be added: music, dance, plastic arts, design, sports teachings, which exceed 11,500 enrolled. In addition to meeting and training this number of pupils and students, it is also necessary to address the coordination of basic complementary services such as school transport; a fully free school transport that uses approximately 90,000 pupils and students from 784 public centers. In turn, the Council signed agreements with 13 entities to facilitate the transport service to 355 students with special educational needs. Galicia is the most autonomous community you invest in in this service in Spain, which, as you know, this year began to lend itself in a shared way on some routes. The system is functioning normally, the school transport is shielded, and the incidences of the start of the course have been resolved with speed. A school luncher based on family income payment is also a great complementary service. In the 2017-2018 course, the number of users of this service is 74,828 students; i.e. 2,193 users more than in the 2016-2017 course, adding the dining rooms managed directly by the Council, the Anpas and the municipalities. The Board manages 434 dining rooms, with more than 60,000 users; directly, 302 dining rooms, and indirectly, through companies that serve menus with all guaranteed quality standards, other 132. For their part, the Anpas are responsible for 134 dining rooms and the municipalities of 42. In addition, the Council signed agreements with 12 entities to facilitate the dining service to 436 students with special educational needs. It is also significant an important element of support for families such as solidarity freeness for the acquisition of textbooks and school materials. In this course 2017-2018 a total of 27,868 tips were granted for the purchase of textbooks, and 99,167 tips for the comparison of school materials. Furthermore, the solidarity fund of textbooks distributed a total of 636,308 manuals, of which it was necessary to acquire only 12.6%. Finally, the Council of Culture, Education and Universitary Order arrived this year, with the program of solidarity freeness for textbooks, almost 137,137 pupils; well through the background, 109,269, well of aid, 27,868, in a joint investment of approximately EUR 12.4 million. The previous three lines of the donation, contribute to the fact that the start of the course is, without a doubt, smoother for families, and much more equitable and just socially. Ladies and gentlemen, we have in Galicia a network of more than 1,380 educational centres, most of which are 100 per cent public, in particular 1,091 centers. That is, about 8 out of every 10 are totally public, centers that offer training to 75% of the student enrolled in our community. As a result of state legislation promoted by the Socialist Party, the network of public-funded centres also includes the concerted. Thus, concerted education responds to approximately 25% of the needs of school squares, dedicated to a budget of around 11%. In addition, it should be noted that almost 90% of the total budget for concerts is intended to pay the salaries of teaching staff in a delegated payment scheme, as well as additional staff in special education units. In this 2017-2018 course there are a total of 3,913 concerted units, 21 more than in the previous course, which provide educational service to around 86,000 students. In any case, it should be noted that the dynamics of the increment of concerted units have decreased significantly in recent years. In fact, in eight years, the current Government of the Union has increased by 77 the number of concerted units, while the Bipartite, in four years, has increased by 213. On the other hand, the Council reviewed, like every year, the enrolment of public schools at all levels; a regular process that, like every year, takes place before the start of each course, in order to adapt the number of units to that of pupils and students enrolled in each educational center. According to the final enrolment data, in this school, 9 unit schools were no longer provided, due to the low level of student in these schools. It must be noted that the council maintains, one more year, the same criterion for suppressing them; that is, it contains 5 or fewer students. As with each course, the reorganization of units within the educational centres was also addressed; as is usual, it was carried out depending on the student's increase or decrease. Even so, units have been enabled for the alleged breakdown of classes when there is a punctual or circumstantial increase in the number of students and students. The Council therefore organised an efficient and efficient use of resources, always taking into account the educational needs of the centres. By the way, in speaking of this school course, I would like to mention that, despite their criticisms and accusations of closure of educational institutions, the reorganization of several centres promoted by the Council is functioning without an incidence. Once the data on services, students, centres and teachers have been offered, it seems appropriate to present the situation of the educational model launched in Galicia over the past few years. Today, when the course has been running for more than two months, we can analyze how this commitment has been realized. Plurilinguism. In this course, we reinforce the commitment to enable language learning to be accessible to the whole of the student of Greece, regardless of their social, territorial or economic situation. The Union has activated for this course the Greek foreign language strategy EDUlingue 2020, whose fundamental objective is to extend multilingualism to all educational stages and to make it easier for students and students to believe their level in foreign language at the end of the stage. Until the past course, plurilingual teaching was implanted in the primary and secondary stages, but from this Iraqi course extending to childhood, bachelor's degree and vocational training. Today, Galicia has 322 multilingual centres; 83 centres for the first time extend multilingualism to children; 400 also for the first time introduce multilingualism to Bachelor; and there are 12 cycles of multilingual vocational training in seven centres in Galicia. Bilingual sections amount to 4.145, 231 more than the previous course. And we have 550 auxiliaries of conversation in this course. Ultimately, multilingualism as a foreign language learning strategy means giving students more opportunity in the future and promoting equal opportunities. Digital education. Under the Galician Strategy for Digital Education, Eurodigital 2020, the Government of Greece develops actions aimed at equipping equipment that enables digital classes and developing digital training with the use of content and the digital book, as well as the reinforcement of digital and scientific-technology competition and STEM studies. The aim is to equip the student of Greek knowledge, skills, skills and skills to manage in an increasingly digital, technological and scientific world. In this course, Galicia's teaching strengthened the Digital Book Project by adding 60 new centers, with an increase of 50%, to reach a total of 154 educational centers supported by public funds. The provision of digital equipment benefits nearly 12,000 students, from 5th primary to 2nd ESO, who are receiving a completely digital teaching. Within the framework of the Digital Project developed by Amtega and the Council, the Board incorporates in this course a third provider of digital content for subjects that are shared between the fifth primary and second secondary ESO. We've already solved the competition, and once I've solved the bidding process, we'll add a quarter to the English subjects of these educational levels. Also in the bidding process is enabling a space for teachers to develop their own digital educational content. There are currently 3,000 available to the teacher, and we want to significantly expand the device of materials created by the teachers themselves. A specific training plan is developed in schools for teachers on the digital book. For access to the digital book to be effective between December and January, the progressive distribution of approximately 14,000 computers for students, touch computers and converted into tablets will be carried out, and 2,000 new computers for teachers, which will renew the equipment they currently have. And during 2018 100 percent of public primary and secondary schools will have at least 100 mega internet access, fruit in this case of the Galician Union Convention with the Government of Spain. Along with this, the Council brought out a roadmap to promote the so-called STEM vocations: science, technology, engineering and mathematics, such as the introduction of robotics in primary, one of the main innovations for this 2017-2018 course, which brings with it the staffing of specific kit centers. In addition, in this area, free configuration materials such as programming, robotics or digital identity — the latest in this course — as well as other specific actions: science club, STEM week, etc., were also launched, with the ultimate aim of fostering the scientific and technological spirit of the student. Co-existence. Living together in education centers is another strategic line of this board. For some time now, we've been committed to the need for further progress in this area to correct any harmful conduct that is detected. Unfortunately, zero risk in harassment or in problems of coexistence does not exist, but Galicia has plans of center-level coexistence and different protocols—transsexuality, against harassment or cyber harassment, TDH, autism, home care, etc.—to know how to act in each case. Last week, the Galicia School Convivence Council analysed the actions grouped into the Galician Strategy of Coexistence and cohabitation.gal, which is our roadmap on this subject. Through its 5 axes and 37 actions, the Strategy has been shown to be an element of planning and operation that gives coherence and security to educational communities. According to the 2015 macroeconomic survey – the biggest survey, which we know, that has never been done in an educational system in Spain and we would dare tell ourselves in Europe – we know that the situation in coexistence is positive in the centres of Galicia. This macroeconomic survey, 200,000 questionnaires and practically 20 million processed data give us very interesting and very significant data: about 85% of families report that their children have not had any conduct contrary to coexistence, or that more than 80 percent of families are satisfied with the centre at which their children attend. Still, it seems appropriate to assess the current situation to deal with the reinforcement of actions already established and the implementation of other news. The more real and objective the diagnosis is, the more successful the policies we implement on this earth can be. In the first quarter of 2018 we will face the realization of a new macro-questionary to test the situation of coexistence in the Italian education, which aims to update the data, both at Galicia level and at the level of each educational center. We intend to establish a biannual periodicity of this survey, which will allow us to take the pulse of coexistence continuously over time and to know its evolution. In order to have as broad a view as possible, the result of the new survey should also serve as a new diagnostic tool for the situation of co-education and the fight against gender violence, as well as for misuse or the risks of digital technologies. As you know, we also aim to boost and activate schools of parents and mothers as forums for debate, information and creation of frameworks of common action around education, where families also have a high role. In this case, Junta will provide the necessary infrastructure through the teacher training centres — the CCRs — as well as expert staff, and will enable a technology tool to store materials at the disposal of families. A catalogue of topics will also be proposed for discussion, either for families, for families and teachers or for schools themselves, which may propose those topics that they consider to be of interest to incorporate into schools. Current vocational training offers a solid general basis and a specialisation according to the labour market and practical knowledge of new technologies. One of the keys to these studies is the connection between the educational world and the productive world, which is to ensure that training at the educational center is combined with training at companies. Today — and after the work that has been carried out in recent years in a genuine silent revolution — Greek vocational training is quality, dynamism, innovation, employability, and a possibility to continue to be formed throughout life; that is, it has prestige. This has been achieved through measures such as the adaptation of 100 cycles to the development of Galician social and economic fabric and its needs; through the consolidation of a network of business farmers in the educational centres themselves that offer the opportunity to create their own businesses and businesses to pupils; the implementation of foreign languages — in particular English — in vocational training in a progressive manner — we have begun in this course; and, among other things, through the extension of the FCTs — from training in work centres — that this year reaches and covers about 15,200 pupils. In order to continue to foster a closer relationship between the educational centre and the company, the Council is firmly committed to dual vocational training. At the moment more than a hundred companies are involved in this project, some of the country's most comprehensive ones, because it is a form of training that alters learning in the educational centre and in the company, which promotes the inclusion of young people and responds to the needs of the productive sectors. We need to keep going deep. In this new course there are 29 new cycles, there are 2 double titrations, and we're still working with the entrepreneurship team to trust in this type of educational training. Given the need for modernization and technology of these teachings, we are working on a vocational training strategy that will pursue an integral vision from the Galician Union and will also encourage the participation of the productive sectors. But the Greek educational model is also supported in other actions that are giving good results, both in the improvement of reading understanding, fundamental to ensuring learning, and in the achievement of more equitable and inclusive teaching. Thus, it is worth mentioning in particular the LÍA Plan of School Libraries for the period 2016-2020, which deepens the need to provide educational centres of useful, stable, sustainable and according to the needs of the 21st century. Among the actions programmed under the LIA Plan is the fact that 646 educational centres across Galicia are already participating in the known Plambe, Plan for the Improvement of School Libraries, after this course there were 36 new incorporations to this initiative, with an investment of approximately €1.3 million. The Improving Plan seeks to encourage and promote the development of school library projects that seek to dynamize this space as a resource centre for reading, information and learning. Even so, the libraries integrated in Plambe are working on designing and developing programs for the acquisition of information and media skills, as well as integrating new technologies and the concept of multiple literacy in addressing the student's reading training. On the other hand, this library model fosters an inclusive approach to the whole of the students. The Council wants to focus on the importance of libraries offering opportunities to all students, especially students with specific educational support needs. Therefore, under the Inclusive Library initiative, we will activate several initiatives focused specifically on supporting libraries of special education centres or those centres that have special education units created or enabled, so that they can count on the resources needed to meet the needs of their student. And, of course, we have to assess to its proper extent the fact that today the whole of the Greek education system is the most inclusive of all Spain. In Galicia, 1 out of every 10 professionals in education are specialists in caring for diversity. I want to finish this first intervention by mentioning the current situation of ABAU because of the importance it has for a very significant number of Thursdays. The first one, which came to colleges with the already started classes, and that was detrimental to the student and made it difficult for the teaching organization to attend classes. In addition, this also allows for a better organization in families and offers improvements to all that has to do with family reconciliation. As is logical, the Council will work to adapt the calendar of the extraordinary test for the 2nd Bachelor's degree student to the dates scheduled for the completion of the access test, in such a way that the non-university and university teaching calendars are accommodated. In addition, it will work to get the three universities to start classes on the same date, which will be set on the second week of September. In other words, the ABAU of the year 2018 is almost as it is, in ABAU 2019 if there are any changes, moving forward to July the extraordinary test that has been carried out in a traditional way in September. Ladies and gentlemen, as you can see, the Greek education system enjoys good health and remains active in adapting to the new challenges that every day emerges; challenges that need to be addressed with an innovative perspective, with planning and with courage, with a ultimate objective: to have a quality, equitable education system that gives opportunities and is socially fair. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u234	Good afternoon. On November 13, 2002, the oil tanker Prestige, loaded with 77,000 tons of fuel, suffered an accident 30 miles from the back of Galicia. The lack of political leadership, neglect, incompetence of Spanish and Greek governments in managing this accident has made it the greatest economic, ecological and social catastrophe in our history. Unfortunately, it is a normal fact that Galicia remains unprotected, orphaned by the Spanish Government's obligations or suffering the consequences of measures taken by it that adversely affect it, but few times in recent history the evidence was so clear to the whole population, even to the most skeptical, and the consequences were so serious. The black tide caused by Prestige affected areas of immense ecological, landscaped, wildlife and extraordinary richness of fish, molluscs and crustaceans of great commercial value. The 63,000-ton balance of sea- poured fuel, nearly 3,000 kilometres of affected coastline, more than 1,000 beaches full of chapotee and an animal mortality of between 115,000 and 200,000 seabirds, was an enormous economic impact of loss of income in the affected areas, both men and women of the sea and other related activities. No one could understand — and still does not understand and less accept — that on one of the coasts facing thousands of ships with dangerous goods and with such important fishing and seafood production there was not and still there was not a corridor far enough away from the coast for ships carrying dangerous goods, there were no powerful tugs, there were no boats to collect the crude, there was no contingency plan, etc. It's hard to understand this degree of discrimination and neglect when there have been such serious accidents on our backs, accidents and disasters that can happen again, because the yellow coasts remain tremendously unprotected and defenceless. Popular of Galicia, at the initiative of Mr César Manuel Fernández Gil and six other Members, on the actions to be carried out by the Council of Galicia to advance the conservation and dissemination of our traditional toponymy
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u254	This motion for a non-law amendment by the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block of Wales was tabled.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u255	To formulate the non-law proposal has the floor to Mrs. Antón Vilasánchez.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2017-12-04-DSPG051.u256	Thank you, President. Ladies and gentlemen, good afternoon. The study, the research, the recovery of the toponyms is a global concern. The proof of this is the existence since 1960 of a group of experts within the UN with the aim of promoting the normalization of geographical names and promoting the national and international benefits that result in such standardization. Their work is fundamental because it facilitates the formulation and dissemination of the right principles, policies and methods to solve the problems of coherence in the use of toponyms, encouraging the recovery of names that are used locally as well as their storage and dissemination. In 2014 this group established the criteria for assessing geographical names as elements of cultural and intangible heritage, declaring that the toponyms are an integral part of the intangible cultural heritage, such as—textually— "constitute essential components of languages and preserve forms that show their evolution, because they are valuable witnesses to history and past and current life modes". Our Autonomous Community is not ally with this concern, because the interest in preserving and recovering toponyms joins in doing so in the language that is our own, particularly after the long night of stone. With the support of Article 5 of the Autonomy Statute, establishing that the language of Galicia is Galicia's own language, the Law of Linguistic Standardization of 1983 goes a little further by stating that the language of Galicia is the only official form for Galician toponyms, a law that has already introduced the concept of administrative co-responsibility in determining the names of geographical places. In this way, the Galician Union is responsible for determining the official names of municipalities, territories, population nuclei, interurban channels of communication and Galician toponyms. On the other hand, the law reserves for the municipalities the determination of official names for urban roads in their municipal term. In this same time frame we must refer to the regulation of the Committee on Toponyms, the body of study, counseling and consultation of the Board of Galicia, heir to the Pre-Autonomic Commission, where persons in the category of Filgueira Valverde, Antonio Fraguas, Carballo Calero, Rio Barja, among others, began the urgent task of returning to its dark form the toponymous wealth of Galicia, freeing it from the distortions or discredited graphics imposed by the oblivion or ignorance, or by the ignorance of our cultural and linguistic heritage, as it collects the exhibition of motives. If we had to put on the table some of the work done from Autonomy up to here, I think that all — and I in particular — would highlight the nominators of the four Greek provinces: Ourense in 1996, Pontevedra in 1998, Lugo in 2000 and, finally, La Coruña in 2003. We can say that, thanks to these works, we have more than 41,000 revised toponyms and 3,523 carefully studied. As the president of the Royal Galaga Academy himself says, the larger toponyms, the important ones, which refer to municipalities, parishes, villages, are already at this first stage, but it's not enough. All the Greek governments have been conscious, we are aware that it does not cease to promote projects in search of the work initiated in the Pre-Self-Governing State. But, undoubtedly, in my opinion, a central and prominent role in this issue plays the Toponyms project of the Galician Union, which starts in 2000 and is set up as a cross-sectional project of different departments of the autonomous administration, but also other administrations, particularly the councils, as well as civil society in general, either individually or collectively through neighborhood associations, cultural associations, mountain communities, etc. And here I also want to name the work done by the institutions, by statutory institutions such as the Galeg Culture Council or the Royal Gallega Academy, the Galician Language Institute, the Father Sarmiento Institute and the universities of Galicia, among others. The different projects and programs developed in thirty-eight years now allow us to have a large volume of information on the toponymity of thousands of places and other geographic realities, such as sodas, frags, areas, streams, fountains..., studied in writing and located on the map, becoming, thanks to modern technologies, essential elements for developing urban planning, cartographic studies, new communication infrastructures, emergency management... but above all, it allows us to obtain valuable information about our culture and our history. The appreciation of toponymity as a cultural heritage implies, in our view, a qualitative leap in its study, in its conservation and in its promotion and dissemination, which is reflected in the latest regulations. At the state level, the Law for the Safeguarding of Inmaterial Cultural Heritage, of the year 2015, which obliges public authorities to preserve traditional toponymity as an instrument of correction, of realization of the geographic designation of the territories. At the autonomous level, Galician Cultural Heritage Act, approved in this House last year, which introduces a specific regulation for intangible heritage, with differentiated treatment for the Greek language and toponymity among others. The law itself determines that the Union of Galicia will, together with other institutions of the Autonomous Community, watch for the preservation of traditional toponyms and pay special attention to the Way of Santiago and its toponyms as part of its values. This law follows closely the spirit of the recommendations of the Convention for the safeguarding of the intangible cultural heritage of the United Nations body, recognizing an active role for communities in the protection and maintenance of cultural and intangible heritage. This recognition obliges us to create a great consensus and weave collaborative networks that will enable us to continue with the identification, documentation, research, protection, promotion, valorization and revitalization of our toponymous heritage. It's an ingenious job that requires large-scale social involvement. In our opinion, public administrations are responsible for creating the avenues, tools and tools needed to weave that great network of civil society that allows us to document all the toponymous wealth in our country. The Galician Toponymy project has already focused on this issue, just like the GeoArpad initiative, a project — you know for sure — of the northern Galician euro area of Portugal that is developing, for example, a computer application, "named Galicia", conceived as an online collaborative tool for collecting, introducing and georeferenced Greek microtoponyms. As part of this project, last week we met one more step in favor of the recovery of our toponymity, putting in place a new program within the Linguistic Dynamics Network leading the General Secretariat of Linguistic Policy: "Toponize, we told you about the names of your land." It is an action of dissemination and awareness that will develop in fourteen municipalities, the hand of recognized specialists, in order to raise awareness of the importance of toponyms and microtoponyms as a heritage resource of incalculable value, because, no doubt, this is the moment of microtoponyms, to prevent the most accurate references of each place, both in its physical and orographic part, of flora, fauna, but, above all, that customary, social part, even imaginable with legends and myths. And it's not easy. The experts explain that Galicia has such a rich toponyms — more than three million toponyms, which is Figueira Valverde — for four reasons: irregular geography — which requires constant reference points —, a continuous settlement in history, intense population dispersion and a fragmentation of property. If we add at present the loss of population in rural areas, especially our greatest guardians of memory, it is clear that this work requires efforts and the help of all to preserve the important collective heritage that we treasure as a society, which is part of our idiosyncrasy and that we have to bind to the generations that succeed us. So today we bring to this House this non-law proposal to urge the Government of Greece to persevere in the design of action that allows the conservation and dissemination of traditional toponymy. And that's why we're going to accept the amendment of the Galician Nationalist Block with a transaction on the original text, as it results in the necessary involvement of all public administrations in this task. Nothing else, thank you very much.
